Indigenous Vaccine Development for Kyasanur Forest Disease (KFD)
• India has initiated Phase-I human clinical trials of an indigenous vaccine for Kyasanur Forest Disease
(KFD), developed with the support of the Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR).
• KFD is a tick-borne viral hemorrhagic fever first identified in the forests of Kyasanur in Karnataka.
• The disease causes high fever, severe weakness, and potentially fatal complications.
• It is mainly confined to the Western Ghats region of India, particularly in Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, Kerala,
Goa, and Maharashtra.
• The disease spreads through the bite of the hard tick Haemaphysalis spinigera and also through direct
contact with infected monkeys.
• KFD does not spread from person to person. The incubation period is about 3–8 days, with symptoms
including high fever, chills, headache, and muscle pain.
• Around 10–20% of patients may develop neurological symptoms in the second stage. There is no specific
antiviral treatment, and care is mainly supportive.
• The mortality rate is estimated at about 3–10%.
• ICMR Established: 1911; Headquarters: New Delhi; Director General: Dr. Rajiv Bahl.
• Western Ghats A UNESCO World Heritage region and biodiversity hotspot known for endemic diseases
and rich biodiversity.
Zimbabwe Launches Lenacapavir Injection for HIV Prevention
• Zimbabwe has launched Lenacapavir for HIV prevention, the first injectable PrEP medicine administered
twice a year.
www.youtube.com/@praveensir 113 GVK Study Circle
=== PDF PAGE 114 ===
• The drug can prevent HIV infection with about 99.9% effectiveness. It is developed by Gilead Sciences
and marketed under the name “Yeztugo”.
• Lenacapavir is a capsid inhibitor that blocks the formation of the virus protein shell (capsid), thereby
preventing viral replication.
• The long-acting injection provides better adherence compared to daily oral pills.
• It represents an important step in Zimbabwe’s HIV control strategy.
• Gilead Sciences Established: 1987; Headquarters: Foster City, California, USA.
• Zimbabwe Capital: Harare; Currency: Zimbabwe Gold (ZiG); President: Emmerson Mnangagwa.
• In Sub-Saharan Africa, where HIV prevalence is high, PrEP is considered an effective prevention strategy
and aligns with the Sustainable Development Goal (SDG-3) for good health and well-being.
Mangrove Clam (Geloina erosa) – ICAR-CMFRI Achieves Success in Induced Breeding
• The ICAR–Central Marine Fisheries Research Institute (CMFRI) has successfully achieved induced
breeding of the mangrove clam in laboratory conditions, a rare scientific achievement globally.
• The mangrove clam belongs to the species Geloina erosa, found in mangrove and estuarine ecosystems of
South and Southeast Asia. In northern Kerala, it is locally known as “Kandal Kakka.”
• The species lives in mud rich in organic matter in intertidal mangrove zones and can tolerate wide
variations in salinity.
• Adult clams usually remain buried deeper in the substrate, while juvenile clams depend less on tidal
influence.
• This success in induced breeding opens new possibilities for conservation, stock enhancement, and
sustainable aquaculture.
• ICAR–Central Marine Fisheries Research Institute Established: 1947; Headquarters: Kochi, Kerala;
Parent body: Indian Council of Agricultural Research (ICAR).
• Kerala Capital: Thiruvananthapuram; Chief Minister: Pinarayi Vijayan.
New Biosurfactant Developed at IASST Under Department of Science and Technology
• A research institute under the Department of Science and Technology in Northeast India has developed a
new biosurfactant using natural materials.
• This substance could serve as an alternative to synthetic surfactants used in cosmetics and pharmaceuticals.
• The compound shows strong antimicrobial properties and is effective against microbes responsible for
skin-related infections.
• It also demonstrates effective cleaning properties. The research was carried out by scientists at the Institute
of Advanced Study in Science and Technology (IASST).
• IASST Established: 1979; Location: Guwahati, Assam; Specialization: Research in chemistry, biological
sciences, and advanced materials.
• Department of Science and Technology Established: 1971; Headquarters: New Delhi.
• Assam Capital: Dispur; Governor: Lakshman Prasad Acharya; Chief Minister: Himanta Biswa Sarma.
• Biosurfactants are environmentally friendly substances that reduce surface tension and have important
antimicrobial applications in the pharmaceutical field.
• Northeast India is emerging as a rapidly developing region in scientific research.

ECONOMY
Union Budget – Meaning and Background
• A budget is an annual statement of revenue and expenditure that provides solutions to the economic and
social challenges faced by the country. It is also called the Annual Financial Statement under Article 112
of the Indian Constitution.
• If the Economic Survey is considered a diagnosis, the Budget can be considered the solution.
• This is the 9th Budget presented by Union Finance Minister Nirmala Sitharaman and the 6th digital Budget
of India.
• The first paperless digital Budget in independent India was presented earlier by Nirmala Sitharaman for
the financial year 2021–22.
• Nirmala Sitharaman is also the woman Finance Minister who has presented the Budget the highest number
of times in India, with 9 presentations.
Budget History
• The first Budget in independent India was presented by R. K. Shanmukham Chetty on 26 November 1947.
• The first Budget in republican India was presented by John Mathai.
• Morarji Desai presented the Budget the highest number of times in India, with 10 presentations.
• Jawaharlal Nehru was the Prime Minister who also presented the Budget while in office.
• Indira Gandhi was the first woman and the first woman Prime Minister to present the Union Budget.
• Nirmala Sitharaman is the woman Finance Minister who has presented the Budget the highest number of
times.
Union Budget 2026–27 – Key Highlights
• Union Finance and Corporate Affairs Minister Nirmala Sitharaman presented the Union Budget 2026–27
in Parliament.
• This is the first Budget prepared in Kartavya Bhavan.
• The Budget has been built around three “Kartavyas” or duties.
Three Kartavyas of the Budget
• First Kartavya: Accelerate economic growth by improving productivity and competitiveness, while
building resilience against global economic uncertainties.
• Second Kartavya: Fulfil people’s aspirations, enhance their capabilities, and make them strong partners
in India’s journey towards prosperity.
• Third Kartavya: Ensure that every family, community, region, and sector has access to the resources,
facilities, and opportunities needed in line with the vision of “Sabka Saath, Sabka Vikas.”
Budget Estimates
• Non-debt receipts are estimated at ₹36.5 lakh crore.
• Total expenditure is estimated at ₹53.5 lakh crore.
• Net tax revenue to the Centre is estimated at ₹28.7 lakh crore.
• Gross market borrowings are estimated at ₹17.2 lakh crore.
• Net market borrowings through dated securities are estimated at ₹11.7 lakh crore.
• In the revised estimates for 2025–26, non-debt receipts are ₹34 lakh crore.
• In the revised estimates, net tax revenue to the Centre is ₹26.7 lakh crore.
• In the revised estimates, total expenditure is ₹49.6 lakh crore.
• Out of this, capital expenditure is around ₹11 lakh crore.

• Fiscal deficit in the revised estimate for 2025–26 stands at 4.4 percent.
• Debt-to-GDP ratio is projected at 55.6 percent in 2026–27, compared to 56.1 percent in 2025–26.
Biopharma and Pharmaceutical Sector Measures
• ₹10,000 crore has been allocated for the next five years under the BioPharma Shakti scheme.
• The objective is to develop India into a global biopharma manufacturing hub.
• Three new National Institutes of Pharmaceutical Education and Research will be established, and the
existing seven institutes will be upgraded.
• A network of more than 1,000 recognized clinical trial centres will be created.

• India Semiconductor Mission 2.0 has been launched.
• Focus will be placed on equipment, raw materials, and development of indigenous intellectual property.
• Industry-linked research and skill development centres will be established.
• Allocation for the electronics components manufacturing scheme has been increased to ₹40,000 crore.
Rare Earths, Chemicals, and Industrial Manufacturing
• Rare earth corridors will be established in Odisha, Kerala, Andhra Pradesh, and Tamil Nadu.
• States will be supported for setting up three dedicated chemical parks.
• Two high-tech tool rooms will be created through CPSEs.
• A manufacturing scheme for construction and infrastructure equipment will be introduced.
• More than ₹10,000 crore has been allocated for five years for a container manufacturing scheme.
Textiles and Fibre Sector Measures
• The National Fibre Mission aims at self-reliance in natural, artificial, and advanced fibres.
• Traditional clusters will be modernized under the Textile Expansion and Employment Scheme.
• Mega textile parks will be established to promote value addition in technical textiles.
• Khadi, handloom, and handicrafts will be strengthened through the Mahatma Gandhi Gram Swaraj
programme.
Industrial Clusters and MSME Support
• A special scheme will be introduced for the revival of 200 legacy industrial clusters.
• A ₹10,000 crore SME Growth Fund will be created for future “Champion SMEs.”
• An additional ₹2,000 crore will be provided to the Atmanirbhar Bharat Fund.
• Professional institutions will be partnered to develop “Corporate Mitras” in Tier-2 and Tier-3 towns.
Capital Expenditure and Infrastructure Measures
• Capital expenditure for 2026–27 has been increased to ₹12.2 lakh crore.
• An Infrastructure Risk Guarantee Fund will be created.
• REITs will be set up for recycling real estate assets of CPSEs.
• New dedicated freight corridors will be developed between Dankuni and Surat.
• Twenty new national waterways will be operationalized within five years.
• Repair systems for river vessels will be set up in Varanasi and Patna.

New Income Tax Law and Simplification Measures
• The new Income Tax law will come into force from April 2026.
• Simplified income tax provisions and forms will be notified soon, and forms will be redesigned to make
compliance easier for ordinary citizens.
• Interest awarded by the Motor Accident Claims Tribunal to an individual will be exempt from income tax,
and TDS on such interest will also be removed.
• TCS on overseas tour programme packages will be reduced to 2 percent from the current range of 2–20
percent.
• TCS under the Liberalised Remittance Scheme for education and medical purposes will be reduced from
5 percent to 2 percent.
• Simplified TDS provisions for manpower supply will be introduced to benefit labour-intensive businesses.
• A rules-based automated process will be introduced for small taxpayers to obtain lower or nil TDS
deduction certificates without applying to the assessing officer.
• Form 15G and 15H for TDS on dividends and interest will be allowed through a single-window filing
mechanism via depositories.
• The revised return filing deadline will be extended from 31 December to 31 March with a nominal fee.
• Return filing timelines will be implemented in a staggered manner.
• In property transactions involving NRIs, PAN-based challans of resident buyers will replace TAN
requirements.
• A one-time six-month foreign asset disclosure scheme will be introduced for small taxpayers to disclose
foreign income or assets.
• Assessment and penalty proceedings will be integrated through a common order.
• Taxpayers will be allowed to update returns even after reassessment begins, subject to payment of an
additional 10 percent tax over the applicable rate.
• In cases of misreporting of income, immunity from penalty may be granted if the additional tax is paid.
• The prosecution framework under the Income Tax Act will be simplified.
• Non-submission of books of account or documents, and TDS obligations in cases where payments are in
kind, will be decriminalized.
• Non-disclosure of non-immovable foreign assets with a value below ₹20 lakh will receive retrospective
immunity from prosecution from 1 October 2024.

Cooperative Sector Tax Relief Measures
• The deduction available to primary cooperative societies supplying milk, oilseeds, fruits, and vegetables
will be extended to societies supplying cattle feed and cotton seeds.
• Inter-cooperative dividend income will be allowed as a deduction only to the extent it is redistributed to
member cooperatives.
• A three-year exemption will be granted on dividends received by notified national cooperative federations
from investments in companies made up to 31 January 2026, provided the amount is distributed to member
cooperatives.
IT Services and Transfer Pricing Measures
• Software development, IT-enabled services, knowledge process outsourcing, and software-related contract
R&D services will be merged into a single category called “Information Technology Services.”
• Safe harbour margin for IT services will be fixed at 15.5 percent.
• The safe harbour threshold for IT services will be raised from ₹300 crore to ₹2,000 crore.
• Safe harbour approval will become a rules-based automated process and may continue for up to five years
at a time.
• Unilateral Advance Pricing Agreements for IT services will be fast-tracked with a target of completion
within two years, extendable by six months if needed.
• The modified return facility available to entities entering into an Advance Pricing Agreement will also be
extended to associated enterprises.
• Foreign companies providing cloud services to global customers through Indian data centre services will
receive a tax holiday until 2047.
• A 15 percent safe harbour on costs will be available where data centre services are provided from India to
related entities.
• Non-residents doing component warehousing in bonded warehouses will get a safe harbour profit margin
of 2 percent on invoice value, resulting in an effective tax of about 0.7 percent.
• Non-residents supplying capital goods, equipment, or tooling to toll manufacturers in bonded zones will
receive a five-year income tax exemption.
• Non-resident experts staying for five years under notified schemes will get exemption on global income.
• Minimum Alternate Tax exemption will be provided to non-residents paying tax under presumptive
taxation.
• A joint committee of the Ministry of Corporate Affairs and the Central Board of Direct Taxes will merge
ICDS into Ind AS.
• From 2027–28, the separate accounting requirement under ICDS will be removed.
• The definition of accountant under safe harbour rules will be simplified.
Buyback, Securities Transaction Tax, and MAT Changes
• Buybacks will be taxed as capital gains for all shareholders.
• Effective tax on buybacks will become 22 percent for corporate promoters and 30 percent for non-
corporate promoters after the additional tax.
• TCS on sellers of liquor, scrap, and minerals will be simplified at 2 percent.
• TCS on tendu leaves will be reduced from 5 percent to 2 percent.
• STT on futures will increase from 0.02 percent to 0.05 percent.
• STT on options premium and exercise will be increased to 0.15 percent.
• Old MAT credit set-off will be allowed only to companies moving to the new tax regime.

• Under the new regime, MAT credit set-off will be allowed only up to one-fourth of tax liability.
• MAT will be converted into a final tax from 1 April 2026, and no new MAT credit accumulation will be
allowed thereafter.
• MAT final tax rate will be reduced from 15 percent to 14 percent.
• Accumulated MAT credit up to 31 March 2026 will continue to be set off as specified.
Customs and Export-Import Reforms
• Duty-free input limits for seafood exports will be raised from 1 percent to 3 percent of FOB value.
• Duty-free import of inputs will be allowed for leather and synthetic footwear exports.

extended.

• Customs duty exemption on imports needed for nuclear power projects will be extended until 2035.
• Basic customs duty exemption will be provided for imports of capital goods required for critical minerals
processing.
• For bio-gas blended CNG, the full value of biogas will be excluded from central excise calculations.
• Basic customs duty exemption will be granted for parts required for civilian and training aircraft
manufacturing.
• Customs duty exemption will also apply to raw materials for aircraft parts used in MRO needs of defence
sector units.
• Specific parts used in microwave oven manufacturing will be granted basic customs duty exemption.
• A one-time special measure will allow SEZ units to sell into the domestic tariff area at concessional duty
rates, up to a specified percentage of exports.
• Customs duty on taxable goods imported for personal use will be reduced from 20 percent to 10 percent.
• Basic customs duty exemption will be granted on 17 medicines and pharmaceutical products.
• Seven more drugs and nutrition products for rare diseases will be allowed duty-free personal import.
Customs Process Reforms and Trade Facilitation
• Faster cargo movement with minimal intervention will be introduced in customs procedures.
• Duty deferment period for Tier-2 and Tier-3 Authorized Economic Operators will be increased from 15
days to 30 days.
• Validity of Advance Rulings will be extended from 3 years to 5 years.
• Government agencies will be encouraged to use Authorized Economic Operator recognition for granting
cargo clearance priority.
• Trusted importers will receive faster clearance through bill of entry filing and auto-notification upon cargo
arrival for non-compliance goods.
• The warehousing system will be transformed into an operator-centric model with self-declarations,
electronic tracking, and risk-based audits.
• Before the end of the financial year, cargo clearance approvals of various agencies will be processed
through a single digital window.
• Clearances for food, drugs, plant, animal, and wildlife products will be brought under this system by April
2026.
• Goods not requiring compliance clearances will be released immediately after online registration is
completed.

• The customs integrated system will be converted into a single unified platform within two years.
• Non-intrusive scanning using advanced imaging and artificial intelligence will be expanded with the goal
of scanning every container at all major ports.
• Fish caught by Indian fishing vessels in the EEZ or high seas will be made duty-free, and landing them at
foreign ports will be treated as exports.
• The ₹10 lakh limit on courier exports will be removed entirely.
• Baggage clearance rules for international travel will be revised and duty-free allowances will be increased.

instead of facing penalties.
Summary of the 16th Finance Commission Report
• Union Finance Minister Nirmala Sitharaman presented the 16th Finance Commission Report in Parliament
along with the Union Budget on 1 February 2026, and the government accepted the key recommendations.
• The Finance Commission recommended that the states’ share in the divisible pool of central taxes should
continue at 41 percent for the period 2026 to 2031, in line with the approach followed by the 15th Finance
Commission.
• The divisible pool refers to the amount remaining after deducting cesses, surcharges, and collection
charges from the Centre’s gross tax revenue.
• Continuing the 41 percent share provides stability in fiscal transfers to states and strengthens the principles
of constitutional fiscal federalism.
• In the matter of horizontal devolution among states, the Commission revised the weights of existing criteria
and added a new parameter to rebalance the distribution formula.
Additional Information on the 16th Finance Commission
• The Finance Commission is a constitutional body established under Article 280 of the Indian Constitution.
• The award period of the 16th Finance Commission is from 2026 to 2031.
• The Chairman of the 16th Finance Commission is Arvind Panagariya.
• The Finance Commission makes recommendations on tax devolution between the Centre and the states,
grants-in-aid, and measures to strengthen state finances.
• Vertical devolution means the share of states in central taxes, while horizontal devolution means the
distribution of that share among the states.

Changes in the 16th Finance Commission and Their Impact on States
• Union Finance Minister Nirmala Sitharaman announced the acceptance of the 16th Finance Commission
recommendations and explained the tax devolution formula for states for the period 2026–31.
• Under vertical devolution, the states’ share in the divisible pool of central taxes has been retained at 41
percent. The Commission clarified that this could not be increased because of the Centre’s difficult fiscal
position.
• The divisible pool is the amount left after deducting cesses, surcharges, and collection charges from the
Centre’s gross tax revenue.
• In horizontal devolution, important changes were made by readjusting the weights of various criteria.
• Greater priority has been given to current population by increasing the population weight to 17.5 percent.
• The importance given to area has been reduced, with the weight for area brought down to 10 percent.
• The weight assigned to income distance has been reduced to 42.5 percent, thereby lowering its relative
influence.
• The weight for forest and ecology has been retained unchanged at 10 percent.
• The new formula partly addresses the concern that southern states, which had performed better in
population control over the years, were being disadvantaged under earlier formulas.
• As a result, Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, Kerala, Tamil Nadu, and Telangana are likely to receive higher
allocations.
• The report also identified the gradual reduction in the size of the divisible pool as a major concern.
• In 2014–15, the divisible pool accounted for 89.1 percent of the Centre’s gross tax revenue, but during
2020–24, this fell to around 74 to 80 percent.
• The main reason for this decline is the increasing dependence of the Centre on cesses and surcharges,
which belong entirely to the Centre and are not shareable with the states.
Additional Information on Devolution
www.youtube.com/@praveensir 121 GVK Study Circle
=== PDF PAGE 122 ===
• The Finance Commission is a constitutional body established under Article 280 of the Indian Constitution.
• The award period of the 16th Finance Commission is from 2026 to 2031.
• The Chairman of the 16th Finance Commission is Arvind Panagariya.
• Vertical devolution means tax distribution between the Centre and the states.
• Horizontal devolution means the distribution of the states’ share among different states.
• Cesses and surcharges are not part of the divisible pool.
Higher Weightage for Telugu States Under the 16th Finance Commission
• Compared with the 15th Finance Commission, the 16th Finance Commission has increased the weightage
of the Telugu states in the inter-state distribution of funds. Andhra Pradesh’s weightage has risen from
4.047 percent to 4.217 percent, while Telangana’s weightage has increased from 2.102 percent to 2.174
percent. As a result, Andhra Pradesh gains an additional 0.17 percentage points and Telangana gains 0.072
percentage points, improving their share in central taxes and other grants.
• According to the 16th Finance Commission recommendations, which will come into effect from 1 April
2026, the central government has estimated that Andhra Pradesh will receive ₹64,362.16 crore from central
taxes based on its 4.217 percent share.
• This is ₹7,620.98 crore higher than the ₹56,741.18 crore received in 2025–26. At the same time,
Telangana’s share is expected to increase from ₹29,471.19 crore to ₹33,180.78 crore, giving it an
additional benefit of ₹3,709.59 crore over the previous year.
• In 2026–27, the central government plans to distribute ₹15,26,254.58 crore to all states combined as their
share in central taxes. This is ₹1,24,199.68 crore higher than the ₹14,02,054.90 crore distributed in 2025–
26.
• If the 15th Finance Commission weightage had continued, Andhra Pradesh would have received only
₹61,767.52 crore and Telangana only ₹32,081.87 crore. With the higher 16th Finance Commission
weightage, Andhra Pradesh gains about ₹2,594.64 crore extra and Telangana gains about ₹1,098.91 crore
extra.
• Earlier, in the temporary recommendations for 2020–21, the 15th Finance Commission had given Andhra
Pradesh a weightage of 4.111 percent and Telangana 2.133 percent, which was later reduced for the 2021–
26 period to 4.047 percent and 2.102 percent respectively. The 14th Finance Commission had
recommended 4.305 percent for Andhra Pradesh and 2.147 percent for Telangana.
• Thus, the 16th Finance Commission has given the Telugu states a higher weightage than the 15th Finance
Commission, but slightly lower than the 14th Finance Commission.

• GDP is the total monetary value of all final goods and services produced within a country’s borders.

Goldman Sachs Raises India’s GDP Growth Forecast to 6.9%
• In February 2026, Goldman Sachs raised India’s calendar year 2026 GDP growth forecast from 6.7 percent
to 6.9 percent, an increase of 20 basis points. The revision was attributed to the India–US trade agreement,
under which tariffs on Indian goods were reduced from 25 percent to 18 percent.
• This estimate was based on the fact that India’s exports to final US demand account for around 4 percent
of GDP and export demand elasticity is estimated at about 0.7.
• Goldman Sachs also projected that reduced uncertainty in trade policy would improve private investment
confidence and support a recovery in capital expenditure during CY26. In the external sector, tariff
reductions are expected to lower the current account deficit by around 0.25 percent of GDP, bringing it to
0.8 percent in CY26.
• It also stated that easing trade tensions could improve foreign investment inflows and reduce pressure on
the Indian rupee.
• India’s bilateral trade surplus with the United States doubled from around USD 20 billion in CY15 to
nearly USD 40 billion in CY25. This increase was mainly driven by rising exports in electronics,
pharmaceuticals, and textiles.

New GDP Series – Changes in Growth Estimates
• The GDP base year has been changed from 2011–12 to 2022–23.
• Changes have been made in estimation methods and additional statistical inputs have been incorporated.
• The real GDP growth estimate for 2025–26 has been revised upward from 7.4 percent to 7.6 percent.
• Goods and Services Tax and vehicle registration statistics have been included in the new calculations.
• The Ministry of Statistics and Programme Implementation has released new annual and quarterly national
accounts under the revised series.
• This is the ninth base revision in India’s GDP series.

• Second-quarter growth was revised upward from 8.2 percent to 8.4 percent.
• First-quarter growth was revised downward from 7.8 percent to 6.7 percent.

• The economy may cross the USD 4 trillion mark.

RBI Monetary Policy Committee Keeps Repo Rate Unchanged at 5.25%
• The Reserve Bank of India’s Monetary Policy Committee decided to keep the repo rate unchanged at
5.25% and maintain a neutral monetary policy stance. Along with the repo rate, the reverse repo rate
remains at 3.35%, the Standing Deposit Facility rate at 5%, and the Marginal Standing Facility rate at
5.50%. A cumulative reduction of 125 basis points had been made earlier in February 2025.
• The real GDP growth estimate for the current financial year was raised from 7.3% to 7.4%. Growth for
the next financial year is projected at around 6.9%–7%.
• Inflation has increased from 2.1% to about 3.2% currently, and is expected to remain around 4%–4.2% in
the next financial year.
• The collateral-free loan limit for micro and small enterprises has been increased from ₹10 lakh to ₹20 lakh,
and bank credit support will also be provided to the real estate sector.
• In cases of digital fraud, customers will be given immediate compensation of up to ₹25,000.
• Reserve Bank of India Established: 1935; Headquarters: Mumbai; Nature: Central bank of India. Monetary
Policy Committee Total members: 6 (3 from RBI and 3 nominated by the Central Government).
SBI Report Estimates GDP Growth at 8.1%
• According to an SBI report, India’s GDP growth in the third quarter of FY 2026 could reach around 8.1%.
The Indian economy remains strong, supported mainly by rising rural consumption and increased spending
in urban areas.
• GDP growth for FY 2026 is estimated at about 7.4%.
• The government has changed the GDP base year from 2011–12 to 2022–23 to reflect structural changes
such as digital trade and developments in the services sector.
• State Bank of India Headquarters: Mumbai, Maharashtra; Established: 1955; Chairman: C. S. Setty. GDP
refers to the total value of goods and services produced within a country.
India Revises Base Year of Merchandise Trade Indices to 2022–23
• The Directorate General of Commercial Intelligence and Statistics announced the revision of the base year
of India’s merchandise trade indices to 2022–23.
• The purpose is to measure changes in the prices of India’s exports and imports more accurately.
• The new indices include monthly, quarterly, and annual indices based on major commodity classifications,
international trade classifications, and broad economic categories.
• It also includes Gross, Net, and Income Terms of Trade indices.
• Importance: These indices help analyze export–import price changes, support trade policy formulation,
and assess international trade competitiveness.

• The funding will strengthen the $200 million Climate-Resilient Brahmaputra Integrated Flood and
Riverbank Erosion Risk Management Project approved in October 2023.

9.4% Growth in Pharma Exports – Double-Digit Target by 2026-27
• India’s pharmaceutical exports grew by 9.4% in FY 2024–25, reaching about ₹2.7 lakh crore.
• The industry aims to achieve double-digit export growth by 2026–27. The Indian pharmaceutical sector is
currently valued at around ₹5.4 lakh crore and is projected to reach ₹11.70 lakh crore by 2030.
• India exports medicines to more than 200 countries, with over 60% of exports going to highly regulated
markets.
• The United States accounts for about 34% of India’s pharma exports, while Europe accounts for around
19%.
• The government is focusing on negotiations and resolving regulatory issues with foreign regulatory
authorities to support export growth.

reached a record level of 3577.32 lakh metric tonnes.

India–China Trade Reaches Record Level
• In 2025, trade between India and China reached about $155 billion, registering a growth of around 12%.
• The growth in trade volume was mainly driven by manufacturing, electronics, chemicals, and intermediate
goods.
• China Capital: Beijing; Currency: Renminbi (Yuan); President: Xi Jinping.
• India imports electronics, machinery, chemicals, and pharmaceutical raw materials from China, while
exporting iron ore, petroleum products, organic chemicals, and agricultural goods to China.

Pradesh.

Two Out of Five Cancer Cases Preventable – International Study
• A global study based on 2022 data states that two out of every five cancer cases worldwide are preventable.
• The study analysed 18.7 million cancer cases across 185 countries and identified tobacco use, alcohol
consumption, physical inactivity, obesity, and infections as major preventable risk factors.
• Lung, stomach, and cervical cancers are among the cancers that can largely be prevented through risk
reduction.
• International Agency for Research on Cancer Established: 1965; Headquarters: Lyon, France; Director:
Elisabete Weiderpass.
• World Health Organization Established: 1948; Headquarters: Geneva, Switzerland; Director-General:

equal economic opportunities for women are implemented globally, and gender inequality in employment
continues.
• The report assessed 190 economies and analysed how laws, regulations, and policies affect women’s
economic participation.
• Key indicators include: average legal framework score 67/100, awareness of implementation score 53, and
supportive policy score 47.
• Only about 4% of women worldwide live in countries with nearly full legal equality.

• Indians lost about ₹22,495 crore due to cyber frauds in 2025, slightly lower than ₹22,845 crore in 2024.

• Meghalaya reported the highest number of HIV cases in the region, with about 10,293 people receiving

• Between 2013 and 2025, around 749 HIV-related deaths were recorded in the state.
• Under the HIV & AIDS (Prevention and Control) Act, 2017, HIV testing cannot be conducted without
consent, and patients cannot be forced into treatment.
• Social stigma remains a major barrier preventing many individuals from seeking medical care.
• Field-level staff are conducting awareness programmes to improve detection and treatment.
• The state government has approved a ₹25 crore five-year programme to strengthen HIV control efforts.
• The initiative focuses on improving diagnosis, counselling, treatment, and community outreach.

• Denotified, Nomadic, and Semi-Nomadic tribes have demanded a separate column for their communities
in the 2027 Census.
• The 2027 Census will include caste enumeration for the first time since the 1931 Census.
• The 1931 Census conducted during British rule was the last nationwide caste census.
• Denotified tribes were officially recognised as a separate category after 1952.
• The Ministry of Home Affairs has issued the Gazette notification related to the upcoming census.
• The census is conducted by the Office of the Registrar General and Census Commissioner under the
Ministry of Home Affairs.

community.

• The airport will be built in Saran district and will have a 4,200-metre runway.

Tripartite Agreement on Frontier Nagaland Territorial Authority
• A tripartite agreement was signed to establish the Frontier Nagaland Territorial Authority as part of efforts
to resolve issues in eastern Nagaland.
• The agreement was signed by the Government of India, the Government of Nagaland, and the Eastern
Nagaland People’s Organisation.
• The agreement was concluded in the presence of Nagaland Chief Minister Neiphiu Rio and Union Home
Minister Amit Shah.
• The demand for a separate administrative arrangement for eastern Nagaland has existed for many years.
• The agreement paves the way for establishing a new administrative system in the region.
• The proposed Frontier Nagaland Territorial Authority will function with a mini secretariat.
• It will focus on addressing issues in six districts of eastern Nagaland.
• Improving administrative efficiency, development, and resolution of public issues are the key objectives.
• Eastern Nagaland People’s Organisation — Established: 2010.
• Nagaland Capital: Kohima.

India Among Six Major Countries Responsible for Agricultural Emissions
• India, China, Indonesia, the United States, Thailand, and Brazil together account for about 61% of global
agricultural greenhouse gas emissions.
• Rice cultivation alone contributes about 43% of these emissions.
• Crops such as rice, maize, oil palm, and wheat together account for nearly two-thirds of agricultural
emissions.
• Nutrient-rich crops such as fruits and vegetables generate comparatively lower emissions.
• Cornell University Established: 1865.
• India Capital: New Delhi.
• China Capital: Beijing.

Ban on Sale of Meat Near Schools and Temples – Bihar Government Decision
• The Bihar government has banned the sale of meat near schools, other educational institutions, and
temples.
• The order applies to the sale of meat, fish, and eggs.
• The decision was taken based on public complaints and feedback received during official visits.
• According to the government order, the ban does not oppose the consumption of non-vegetarian food, but
addresses concerns about foul smell causing inconvenience to students and devotees.
• The measure aims to maintain cleanliness and social harmony in such areas.
• Bihar Capital: Patna; Governor: Arif Mohammad Khan; Chief Minister: Nitish Kumar.
National UAV Test and Innovation Corridor to be Established in Odisha

• The corridor will be developed at Rangeilunda Airport in Ganjam district, using the existing commercial

Mandatory 20% Ethanol-Blended Petrol – Central Government Notification
• The Government of India has made the sale of petrol blended with 20% ethanol (E20) mandatory across
all states and Union Territories from 1 April 2026.
• The notification specifies that the petrol must have a Research Octane Number (RON) of 95.
• Temporary exemptions may be granted to certain regions under special circumstances.
• Ethanol is produced mainly from sugarcane and maize, helping reduce environmental pollution.
• The policy will increase crop demand, support farmers, and reduce crude oil imports.
• Vehicles manufactured from 2023 onwards are designed to be compatible with E20 fuel.
• Ministry of Petroleum and Natural Gas Established: 1999; Headquarters: New Delhi; Union Minister:
Hardeep Singh Puri.
New Waste Segregation System Under SWM Rules 2026
• The Solid Waste Management (SWM) Rules 2026 will come into force on 1 April 2026, replacing the
2016 SWM Rules.
• Households must segregate waste into four categories at the source — wet waste, dry waste, sanitary waste,
and special care waste.
• Wet waste includes kitchen and food waste, which must be processed through composting or
biomethanation.
• Dry waste includes recyclable materials that will be sent to Material Recovery Facilities (MRFs) for
segregation.
• Sanitary waste includes items such as diapers and sanitary pads, which must be securely wrapped and
stored separately.
• Special care waste includes bulbs, medicines, and similar items that must be handed over only to
authorized agencies.
• The four-bin system will be used Green for wet waste, Blue for dry waste, Red for sanitary waste, and
Black for special care waste.
• The reform aims to improve environmental protection and make recycling more efficient.
• Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change Established: 1985; Headquarters: New Delhi; Union
Minister: Bhupender Yadav.

DAYS AND THEMES
Indian Coast Guard 50th Raising Day
• The Indian Coast Guard celebrated its 50th Raising Day on 1 February.
• Established in 1977 with only 7 surface vessels, the force has grown into a powerful maritime security
organisation with about 155 ships and 80 aircraft.
• It performs responsibilities such as coastal security, maritime safety, protection of the Exclusive Economic
Zone (EEZ), enforcement of maritime laws, search and rescue operations, and marine environmental
protection.
• As part of future capacity expansion, it aims to achieve a strength of 200 surface vessels and 100 aircraft
by 2030.
• Indian Coast Guard Established: 1977; Formed under the Coast Guard Act, 1978.
• It functions under the Ministry of Defence.
• Headquarters: New Delhi; Director General: Paramesh Sivamani.
World Wetlands Day – February 2
• World Wetlands Day is observed every year on 2 February; Theme for 2026: “Wetlands and traditional
knowledge: Celebrating cultural heritage.”
• Wetlands play a crucial role in biodiversity conservation, flood control, water purification, groundwater
recharge, and climate regulation.
• Wetlands are found on every continent except Antarctica and serve as natural habitats for many plant and
animal species.
• Examples include coastal areas, riverbanks, marshlands, mangrove forests, coral reefs, freshwater lakes,
saltwater lakes, paddy fields, fish ponds, and irrigation reservoirs.
• Under the Ramsar Convention, areas with flowing or stagnant water are identified as wetlands.
• Rapid population growth, urbanisation, and land-use changes are causing wetlands to decline globally.
• The Ramsar Convention on Wetlands was adopted on 2 February 1971 in Ramsar, Iran, and World
Wetlands Day has been observed annually since 1997.
• The Ramsar Convention is an international treaty for wetland conservation, and India joined it in 1982.
• Wetlands are among the most productive ecosystems, and mangrove forests act as natural shields
protecting coastal regions from storms and erosion.
• Loss of wetlands can significantly affect food security, water security, and biodiversity.
World Cancer Day – February 4
• World Cancer Day is observed every year on 4 February to raise awareness about cancer prevention, early
detection, effective treatment, and to unite countries in the fight against cancer.
• The day promotes healthy lifestyle habits, increased screening, and removal of myths and misconceptions
about cancer.
• Cancer is a disease in which certain cells in the body grow uncontrollably and spread to other parts of the
body; it can develop in almost any organ.
• Normally, cell division occurs in a regulated manner for growth and repair of the body; when this control
fails, abnormal cell groups form and develop into cancer.
• On 4 February 2000, the World Cancer Summit for the New Millennium was held in Paris, France, where
the Paris Charter Against Cancer was signed; this led to the declaration of World Cancer Day.
www.youtube.com/@praveensir 140 GVK Study Circle
=== PDF PAGE 141 ===
• Union for International Cancer Control Established: 1933; Headquarters: Geneva, Switzerland; CEO:
Cary Adams; Global organisation promoting cancer prevention and control.
• World Health Organization — Established: 1948; Headquarters: Geneva, Switzerland; Director-General:
Dr. Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus; Specialised public health agency of the United Nations.
World Pulses Day – February 10
• World Pulses Day is observed every year on 10 February; Theme: “Pulses of the World: From Modesty
to Excellence.”
• Pulses are an important plant-based source of protein and play a key role in ensuring nutritional security.
• They belong to the Leguminosae family and improve soil fertility through nitrogen fixation, supporting
sustainable agriculture.
• Consumption of pulses helps reduce the risk of heart disease, regulate blood sugar levels, and control
obesity.
• Due to their high protein content and low cost, pulses are often called the “protein of the poor.” Common
pulses include pigeon pea (tur), black gram (urad), green gram (moong), chickpeas, and masoor dal.
• According to the World Health Organization, an average person should consume about 80 grams of pulses
per day.
• Recognizing their importance, the United Nations declared 2016 as the International Year of Pulses in
2013.
• Later, in December 2018, the UN General Assembly declared 10 February as World Pulses Day, observed
annually since 2019.
• United Nations — Established: 1945; Headquarters: New York, USA; Secretary-General: António
Guterres; Nature: International intergovernmental organisation.
• World Health Organization — Established: 1948; Headquarters: Geneva, Switzerland; Director-General:
Dr. Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus; Specialized UN public health agency.
World Radio Day Conclave 2026 in Raipur
• On 13 February 2026, All India Radio organised the World Radio Day Conclave in Raipur, the capital of
Chhattisgarh, in collaboration with UNESCO.
• Theme: “Radio and Artificial Intelligence”; UNESCO highlighted the theme line “AI is a tool, not a
voice.”
• Discussions focused on how Artificial Intelligence is transforming newsrooms, content creation,
automation, multilingual broadcasting, accessibility, and maintaining public trust in radio.
• World Radio Day is observed globally every year on 13 February.
• All India Radio — Adopted the name on 8 June 1936; National public broadcaster of India under Prasar
Bharati.
• Prasar Bharati CEO: Gaurav Dwivedi (IAS).
• UNESCO Headquarters: Paris, France; Director-General: Khaled El-Enany (from 15 November 2025).
• Chhattisgarh Capital: Raipur; Chief Minister: Vishnu Deo Sai; Governor: Ramen Deka.
National Women’s Day – February 13
• National Women’s Day is observed in India every year on 13 February.
• The day marks the birth anniversary of Sarojini Naidu, a renowned poet and freedom fighter.
www.youtube.com/@praveensir 141 GVK Study Circle
=== PDF PAGE 142 ===
• Sarojini Naidu is regarded as a symbol of women’s empowerment and independent spirit and is popularly
known as the “Nightingale of India.”
• The objective of the day is to recognise women’s achievements in cultural, social, political, and economic
fields and highlight their role in nation-building.
• It is different from International Women’s Day, which is celebrated worldwide on 8 March.
• Since 2014, India has observed 13 February as National Women’s Day to honour Sarojini Naidu’s
contributions to women’s empowerment and social justice.
• Sarojini Naidu Born: 13 February 1879; Died: 2 March 1949; Freedom fighter and women’s rights activist.
• She became the first Indian woman President of the Indian National Congress in 1925 and later served as
the Governor of Uttar Pradesh in 1947, becoming India’s first woman governor.
• International Women’s Day Celebrated globally on 8 March to promote women’s rights, equality, and
empowerment.
Global Tourism Resilience Day – February 17
• Global Tourism Resilience Day is observed every year on 17 February to highlight the importance of
resilience in the tourism sector.
• Tourism plays a vital role in national development, employment generation, awareness, and inclusive
growth, relying on culture, heritage, people, arts, cuisine, and destinations.
• Climate change, economic recessions, and pandemics are major disruptions affecting the tourism industry.
• The day focuses on developing strategies to face future disruptions and promote economic growth, cultural
exchange, and environmental sustainability.
• People who travel for leisure or recreation are called tourists, and the places they visit are known as tourist
destinations.
• According to the United Nations World Tourism Organization, a tourist is a person who travels outside
their usual residence for more than 24 hours but less than one year without earning income at the
destination.
• According to the Government of India, a foreign tourist is a person holding a foreign passport who stays
in India for more than one day but less than one year for purposes such as leisure, medical treatment,
religion, or business.
• United Nations World Tourism Organization — Established: 1975; Headquarters: Madrid, Spain;
Secretary-General: Shaikha Al Nowais; Specialized UN agency.
• International tourism is recognised as one of the world’s largest service industries.
• Sustainable tourism refers to tourism practices that minimise environmental impact while preserving local
culture.
• Ministry of Tourism (Government of India) Headquarters: New Delhi; Union Minister: Gajendra Singh
Shekhawat.
• Spain Capital: Madrid; Currency: Euro; Prime Minister: Pedro Sánchez.
International Mother Language Day – February 21
• International Mother Language Day is observed worldwide every year on 21 February.
• It promotes linguistic diversity and multilingual education while highlighting the role of languages in
inclusion, cultural diversity, and achieving sustainable development goals.
• Theme for 2026: “Youth voices on multilingual education.”
www.youtube.com/@praveensir 142 GVK Study Circle
=== PDF PAGE 143 ===
• The theme emphasises that language is not only a tool for communication but also a key element of
identity, education, well-being, and social participation.
• The day aims to promote education in mother languages and protect endangered languages.
• UNESCO declared this observance in 1999, and it has been celebrated annually since 2000.
• The date commemorates the language movement of 1952 in Bangladesh.
• UNESCO Established: 1945; Headquarters: Paris, France; Director-General: Khaled El-Enany;
Specialized agency of the United Nations.
• Sustainable Development Goals Adopted by the United Nations in 2015; Total goals: 17; Target year:
2030.
National Science Day – February 28
• National Science Day is observed every year on 28 February to honour the contributions of scientists to
national development.
• The day commemorates the discovery of the Raman Effect by Indian physicist C. V. Raman in 1928.
• For this discovery, he was awarded the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1930.
• The Government of India declared 28 February as National Science Day in 1986, and the first celebration
was held in 1987.
• Since then, the day has been celebrated annually with a specific theme.
• Theme for 2026: “Women in Science: Catalysing Viksit Bharat.”
• Chandrasekhara Venkata Raman Born: 7 November 1888; Died: 21 November 1970; First Asian scientist
to receive the Nobel Prize in science; Discovered the Raman Effect related to light scattering.
• Nobel Prize Established: 1901; Categories include Physics, Chemistry, Medicine, Literature, Peace, and
Economic Sciences; Awarded annually.
